But I kept coming back to the same thing: this was important, and the block was supposed to become something through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that the neighborhood was chosen for this project and that it would miss its chance if you didn't finish it, despite what others have told you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, and I know people have told me I’m making too much of it. My family especially, they keep saying, “The neighborhood wasn’t chosen, it’s just you working on the house,” and I understand those words. I’m not deaf to it.\n\nBut no, I don’t really believe them. I still think this block was picked for it, or meant for it somehow. Not picked like someone sent me a letter, but picked in the way things line up and you finally see what they’re supposed to be. This was supposed to be my last big civic art piece, something for everybody here, not just for me. And if I don’t finish it, then yes, I do feel like the neighborhood misses the chance. Like the moment passes and people don’t even understand what they lost.\n\nAnd there were signs. It wasn’t just in my head the way they say. Trucks slowed down in front of the house. Drivers looked over more than normal. Neighbors came outside, stood in their yards, watched from their windows. They may not have said, “Yes, this is important,” but you can tell when people know something is happening. They knew. Maybe they didn’t want to admit it, or maybe they got embarrassed when my family started making it sound foolish, but I could see it.\n\nSo yes, despite what others told me, I still believe it. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but I’m sure this neighborhood was supposed to have this, and I was supposed to finish it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people in the neighborhood share your belief that the house and block would miss their chance if the project wasn't finished?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think some of them share it, even if they won’t come right out and say it. That’s the part my family doesn’t understand. They act like if nobody says the words, then nobody believes it, but people don’t always say important things plainly. Sometimes they show it by how they behave.\n\nLike I told you, the trucks slowing down — not just one truck, not just one time — drivers would look over at the house in that way. And neighbors, they’d be out in their yards, or standing near the sidewalk, or looking from the windows. You can tell when people are just being nosy, and you can tell when people sense something bigger is happening. This felt like the second one.\n\nNow, if you asked them directly, maybe they’d deny it. Maybe they’d say, “Oh no, I was just looking,” because people get embarrassed, or they don’t want to be pulled into family business. But I think they knew the block was being given a chance, and that if I didn’t finish it, the house and the neighborhood would lose something. I know people think I’m mixed up about that, but I’m sure this was meant to be my last big civic art piece, and I think the little signs showed that other people understood it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you misinterpreted the signs you mentioned, such as the trucks slowing down and the neighbors looking, and that they didn't actually understand or believe in the importance of your project?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is possible if you put it that way. I mean, I understand what you’re asking me — could the truck driver just have been slowing down because of the street, or looking because there was work going on, or the neighbor just happened to be outside? I know that’s the way my family explains it. They say, “People look at houses all the time,” and “You’re reading into it.” I hear that.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I misinterpreted it. Not in the way they mean. It wasn’t one little thing by itself. It was the pattern of it. The timing. The way people would slow down right there, and look over, and the way neighbors seemed to come out and watch. It felt like they knew this was more than just fixing up a house. They may not have said it out loud, but I could tell they understood something important was happening.\n\nAnd I know people think I’m mixed up about it. I know that. I’m not saying I don’t know how it sounds. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for this, like it was meant to be my last big civic art piece for the block. Those little signs mattered. To me, they proved other people knew it too, even if they won’t admit it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the whole thing isn’t real the way I’m saying it is. And I can understand why you’d ask that, because my family says it all the time — that I’m mixed up, that I’m putting meaning into ordinary things, that the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking me what I believe, no, I don’t really think it’s not based in reality. I think people just don’t see it, or they don’t want to say they see it. To me, it was too many little things lining up. The trucks slowing down in front of the house, the way the drivers looked over, the neighbors watching from their yards or windows — those weren’t nothing. That showed me they knew something was happening too.\n\nI know how that sounds. I’m not trying to be difficult. But I’m sure this neighborhood was picked, or meant, for my last big civic art piece. It wasn’t just me deciding that out of nowhere. The signs were there, and I still believe the block would miss its chance if I didn’t finish it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would change if everyone around you, including the neighbors and truck drivers, told you directly that they didn't understand its importance and weren't paying attention to it?\n\nPatient: If every single one of them said that? I mean… I’d hear them saying it. I’m not saying I’d cover my ears. If the neighbors came right out and said, “No, we weren’t watching for that,” or the truck drivers said, “No, I didn’t know anything about your project,” I would hear the words.\n\nBut I don’t think it would change what I know happened. Not completely. Because people can say things after the fact for all kinds of reasons. They don’t want to get involved, they don’t want to look strange, they don’t want to admit they understood something bigger was going on. Especially when my family is already saying I’m mixed up — people will go along with that because it’s easier.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just one person, Doctor. It was the whole feeling around the block. The slowing down, the looking over, the timing of it, the way it kept happening. That’s what people miss when they ask me like it’s just one neighbor staring out a window. It wasn’t that simple. It felt like the neighborhood had been picked for this, like the house and the block had a chance through my work.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think my belief would just go away because people denied it. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but I’m still sure this was meant to be my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, whether they admit it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would be considered strange or unrealistic by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know most people would probably say it sounds strange. I’m not blind to that. My family already says I’m mixed up about it, and I can hear how it sounds when I say the trucks slowing down or the neighbors looking over meant something. I know a regular person might say, “That’s just traffic,” or “People look at things,” or “Nobody picked the neighborhood for anything.”\n\nBut to me, that doesn’t mean it wasn’t real. It’s just that most people don’t look at the whole pattern the way I did. They take each little thing by itself and explain it away. But when you’re there, day after day, seeing the way people slow down right at the house, the way they glance over, the way neighbors seem to be watching from their yards or windows — it adds up. It felt like they understood this was more than just some little project.\n\nSo yes, I know it would be considered unrealistic by a lot of people. I know that. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t admit it or don’t want to say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project could be a mistaken idea that is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me to leave room for the idea that I could be wrong… and I can say, in a general way, people can be wrong about things. I’m not saying I’ve never misunderstood anything in my life. But with this, no, I don’t really believe it’s just a mistaken idea.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. My family says that, and I can see by the way people react when I explain it that they think I’m reading too much into things. But it wasn’t just one little thing. It was the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, neighbors watching from windows or standing outside at just the right times. When it happens once, maybe you brush it off. When it keeps happening, and it lines up with what I was trying to do with the art piece, it means something.\n\nSo if you’re asking could it be not based in reality… I have a hard time saying yes to that, because to me the signs were there. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. Maybe other people don’t want to admit they understood it, or maybe they don’t see the pattern the way I do, but I still believe they knew something important was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any external evidence that could prove or disprove your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project?\n\nPatient: External evidence… well, that’s hard, because what counts as evidence to me doesn’t seem to count to other people.\n\nIf you mean some official paper saying, “Yes, this neighborhood was selected,” no, I don’t have a paper like that. Nobody handed me a certificate or came to the door and announced it. But that doesn’t mean there wasn’t evidence. The evidence was in what was happening around me — the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors being outside or watching at the right moments. Those are the things I keep coming back to, because they weren’t just random to me. They fit together.\n\nNow, could something disprove it? I suppose someone could try. They could say, “We checked, and nobody knew anything.” But I’ve already said, people can deny things. People don’t always admit when they notice something important, especially if they think they’ll be made to look foolish. And once my family started saying I was mixed up, then of course people would back away from it.\n\nSo I know how it sounds. I know you’re asking for something outside my own mind, something you can put on a table and look at. But from where I was standing, the outside evidence was already there. It was the way the block moved around it, the timing, the attention, the little signs. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those things proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if a professional, such as an art critic or a community leader, told you that your work, although meaningful to you, does not have the broader significance you believe it does?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change it, no. I mean… I would listen, I’m not saying I’d put my hands over my ears. If an art critic or somebody from the community came and said, “Your work matters to you, but it doesn’t mean what you think it means,” I’d hear the words. But I don’t think that would prove they were right.\n\nBecause people can look at something and miss what’s really happening. Especially with a piece like this, where it wasn’t just the object or the painting or whatever they want to call it. It was the whole neighborhood, the way it was being seen, the way people were reacting around it. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t just me deciding one morning that my work was important. It was the trucks slowing down by the house, the drivers turning their heads, the neighbors looking out or standing around at the right times. Those things showed me people knew something was going on.\n\nSo if a professional told me it didn’t have broader significance, I’d probably think they either didn’t understand the full situation, or they were trying to talk me down because my family already said I’m mixed up. I know people think that about me. I know how it sounds. But I’m still sure the neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, whether they admit it or not.\n\nSo no… I don’t think one person’s opinion, even a professional person, would change what I know I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were shown clear evidence that contradicts them?\n\nPatient: If it was truly clear evidence… I mean, I hear what you’re saying. If somebody showed me something solid, something that wasn’t just somebody’s opinion, I’d have to look at it. I’m not trying to be impossible. I don’t want to sit here and say nothing could ever make me think.\n\nBut the problem is, when people say “clear evidence,” a lot of times they mean what they already believe. They mean my family saying I’m mixed up, or somebody telling me, “Those trucks were just driving by,” or “The neighbors were just outside.” And to me, that doesn’t answer it, because I was there. I saw how it happened. The trucks slowing right by the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching or coming out at the times that mattered — it wasn’t just one little thing. It was a pattern.\n\nSo if you showed me something that contradicted it, I’d probably want to know who made it, why they’re saying it, what they left out. Because people can deny things, or they can misunderstand what they saw. And once everybody starts saying I’m confused, then anything I say gets put in that box.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up, I do know that. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. Those little things that happened around it proved to me that people knew it too. So… I can say I would look at evidence, yes. But I don’t know that it would change what I know I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were shown clear evidence that the neighborhood was not, in fact, chosen for anything special?\n\nPatient: I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t think it would change it just because somebody showed me something that says the neighborhood wasn’t chosen. I’d have to look at what they were calling “clear evidence,” because that matters. A piece of paper or somebody saying, “No, nothing special was going on,” doesn’t erase what I saw with my own eyes.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. I know my family and other people look at it like I connected things that weren’t connected. But I was there. It wasn’t just one truck one time, or one neighbor glancing out a window. It was the way things kept happening — trucks slowing down right near the house, drivers looking over like they knew, neighbors being outside or watching at just the right moments. Those little things added up. They showed me people knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if you showed me something saying, “No, the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything special,” I’d probably think maybe whoever wrote it didn’t understand what the project really was, or they were only looking for an official kind of choosing, like a committee or a permit or something. But that’s not the whole of it. The proof was in the attention around it, the timing, the way people acted.\n\nI’m not trying to be stubborn, Doctor. I know how this sounds. But no, I don’t think that kind of evidence would really change what I believe, because I know what I saw happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to experience a significant period of time, say several months, where none of the signs you mentioned, such as trucks slowing down or neighbors watching, occurred?\n\nPatient: Several months with none of it happening… I mean, I’d notice that, sure. I’m not saying I wouldn’t notice. If the trucks stopped slowing down, if the neighbors weren’t looking over anymore, if the whole feeling around the place went quiet, I’d be aware of that. I watch those things because that’s how I knew people understood what was going on.\n\nBut I don’t think it would make me say the neighborhood wasn’t picked. It might make me think that part of it had passed, or people were being more careful, or maybe once everybody knew I was onto it, they stopped showing it so openly. You know, signs don’t have to keep happening every single day for the thing to be real. A thing can happen, and then people back away from it.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up about this. I know that. My family says it, and I can tell from the way people talk to me that they think I put too much meaning into ordinary things. But I’m telling you, when it was happening, it was not ordinary. Those trucks slowing down near the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching at certain times — it all fit together. It proved to me that they knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if months went by and there were no more signs, I might wonder why it got quiet. I might think the attention moved underground, or that people had already gotten the message. But no, I don’t think the quiet afterward would erase what I already saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to receive a professional evaluation stating that your perceptions of the neighborhood's reaction are not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Well… if a professional wrote that, I’d listen to it, I suppose. I’m not saying I’d throw it in the trash or anything like that. I know you all have training, and I know people are trying to make sense of what I’m saying. And I know people already think I’m mixed up about this — my family, neighbors, whoever — so a report like that wouldn’t surprise me exactly.\n\nBut would it change what I believe? I don’t think so. Not just because somebody professional says my perceptions aren’t based in reality. Because they weren’t standing there when it happened. They didn’t see the trucks slowing down by the house, the way the drivers looked over, and they didn’t see how the neighbors were watching or coming out at just the right times. It wasn’t one little thing I misunderstood. It was a whole pattern, and I lived through it.\n\nA professional evaluation might say, “He’s connecting things wrong,” or “Those were ordinary neighborhood things,” but that feels too easy to me. It puts everything in one box without really accounting for the timing and the feeling of it, the way it kept lining up around the project. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand. The little things proved people knew it too.\n\nSo I’d read it, or I’d hear it, and I might even try to think about it. But I don’t think it would take away what I know I saw. It would probably just make me feel like the professional didn’t have the whole picture.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. A piece of paper or somebody saying, “No, nothing special was going on,” doesn’t erase what I saw with my own eyes.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. I know my family and other people look at it like I connected things that weren’t connected. But I was there. It wasn’t just one truck one time, or one neighbor glancing out a window. It was the way things kept happening — trucks slowing down right near the house, drivers looking over like they knew, neighbors being outside or watching at just the right moments. Those little things added up. They showed me people knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if you showed me something saying, “No, the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything special,” I’d probably think maybe whoever wrote it didn’t understand what the project really was, or they were only looking for an official kind of choosing, like a committee or a permit or something. But no, I don’t think that kind of evidence would really change what I believe, because I know what I saw happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to experience a significant period of time, say several months, where none of the signs you mentioned, such as trucks slowing down or neighbors watching, occurred?\n\nPatient: Several months with none of it happening… I mean, I’d notice that, sure. I’m not saying I wouldn’t notice. If the trucks stopped slowing down, if the neighbors weren’t looking over anymore, if the whole feeling around the place went quiet, I’d be aware of that. I watch those things because that’s how I knew people understood what was going on.\n\nBut I don’t think it would make me say the neighborhood wasn’t picked. It might make me think that part of it had passed, or people were being more careful, or maybe once everybody knew I was onto it, they stopped showing it so openly. You know, signs don’t have to keep happening every single day for the thing to be real. A thing can happen, and then people back away from it.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up about this. I know that. My family says it, and I can tell from the way people talk to me that they think I put too much meaning into ordinary things. But I’m telling you, when it was happening, it was not ordinary. Those trucks slowing down near the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching at certain times — it all fit together. It proved to me that they knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if months went by and there were no more signs, I might wonder why it got quiet. I might think the attention moved underground, or that people had already gotten the message. But no, I don’t think the quiet afterward would erase what I already saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to receive a professional evaluation stating that your perceptions of the neighborhood's reaction are not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Well… if a professional wrote that, I’d listen to it, I suppose. I’m not saying I’d throw it in the trash or anything like that. I know you all have training, and I know people are trying to make sense of what I’m saying. And I know people already think I’m mixed up about this — my family, neighbors, whoever — so a report like that wouldn’t surprise me exactly.\n\nBut would it change what I believe? I don’t think so. Not just because somebody professional says my perceptions aren’t based in reality. Because they weren’t standing there when it happened. They didn’t see the trucks slowing down by the house, the way the drivers looked over, and they didn’t see how the neighbors were watching or coming out at just the right times. It wasn’t one little thing I misunderstood. It was a whole pattern, and I lived through it.\n\nA professional evaluation might say, “He’s connecting things wrong,” or “Those were ordinary neighborhood things,” but that feels too easy to me. It puts everything in one box without really accounting for the timing and the feeling of it, the way it kept lining up around the project. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand. The little things proved people knew it too.\n\nSo I’d read it, or I’d hear it, and I might even try to think about it. But I don’t think it would take away what I know I saw.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… yes, I suppose a mental health professional could call them delusions. I understand that word gets used when somebody believes something other people don’t believe, or when people think the person is seeing meaning where there isn’t any. So if you’re asking whether someone in your position might write that down about me, then yes, I can see that happening.

But that doesn’t mean I agree with it. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. I know people think I’m mixed up. I know they hear me talk about the trucks slowing down, or the neighbors looking over, or people seeming to know when to be outside, and they think, “Well, he’s making too much of ordinary things.” I know how it sounds when it’s all put into words in an office like this.

But I was there. I saw how it was happening around the house. It wasn’t just one small thing. It was the pattern of it, the timing, the way it kept lining up. To me, those things proved that people knew the neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece. So, yes, maybe a professional could call it that. I’m not blind to that. But in my mind, it feels like they’d be calling it that because they didn’t really see what I saw.

I watch those things because that’s how I knew people understood what was going on.\n\nBut I don’t think it would make me say the neighborhood wasn’t picked. It might make me think that part of it had passed, or people were being more careful, or maybe once everybody knew I was onto it, they stopped showing it so openly. You know, signs don’t have to keep happening every single day for the thing to be real. A thing can happen, and then people back away from it.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up about this. I know that. My family says it, and I can tell from the way people talk to me that they think I put too much meaning into ordinary things. But I’m telling you, when it was happening, it was not ordinary. Those trucks slowing down near the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching at certain times — it all fit together. It proved to me that they knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if months went by and there were no more signs, I might wonder why it got quiet. I might think the attention moved underground, or that people had already gotten the message. But no, I don’t think the quiet afterward would erase what I already saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to receive a professional evaluation stating that your perceptions of the neighborhood's reaction are not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Well… if a professional wrote that, I’d listen to it, I suppose. I’m not saying I’d throw it in the trash or anything like that. I know you all have training, and I know people are trying to make sense of what I’m saying. And I know people already think I’m mixed up about this — my family, neighbors, whoever — so a report like that wouldn’t surprise me exactly.\n\nBut would it change what I believe? I don’t think so. Not just because somebody professional says my perceptions aren’t based in reality. Because they weren’t standing there when it happened. They didn’t see the trucks slowing down by the house, the way the drivers looked over, and they didn’t see how the neighbors were watching or coming out at just the right times. It wasn’t one little thing I misunderstood. It was a whole pattern, and I lived through it.\n\nA professional evaluation might say, “He’s connecting things wrong,” or “Those were ordinary neighborhood things,” but that feels too easy to me. It puts everything in one box without really accounting for the timing and the feeling of it, the way it kept lining up around the project. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand. The little things proved people knew it too.\n\nSo I’d read it, or I’d hear it, and I might even try to think about it. But I don’t think it would take away what I know I saw. It would probably just make me feel like the professional didn’t have the whole picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project could be considered delusions by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose a mental health professional could call them delusions. I understand that word gets used when somebody believes something other people don’t believe, or when people think the person is seeing meaning where there isn’t any. So if you’re asking whether someone in your position might write that down about me, then yes, I can see that happening.\n\nBut that doesn’t mean I agree with it. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. I know people think I’m mixed up. I know they hear me talk about the trucks slowing down, or the neighbors looking over, or people seeming to know when to be outside, and they think, “Well, he’s making too much of ordinary things.” I know how it sounds when it’s all put into words in an office like this.\n\nBut I was there. I saw how it was happening around the house. It wasn’t just one small thing. It was the pattern of it, the timing, the way it kept lining up. To me, those things proved that people knew the neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece. So, yes, maybe a professional could call it that. I’m not blind to that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… “ever” is a big word. I mean, I suppose if you’re asking me in a very plain way, could something make me question it, I can say maybe I would think about it if there was some real explanation for all of it that made sense. Not just somebody saying, “No, that didn’t happen,” because that doesn’t answer what I saw.

The thing is, I already know people think I’m mixed up about it. I’m not pretending I don’t know that. I can hear it in how my family talks to me, and I can hear it here too, the way the questions are asked. People think I took ordinary things — a truck slowing down, a neighbor looking over — and made them into something bigger. But to me it wasn’t just one truck, or one glance, or one person outside. It was the way all those little things came together around the house, around the neighborhood, around the idea of the project.

So could I doubt it? Maybe in little moments I could wonder why other people don’t see it the way I do. I can wonder why it sounds strange when I say it out loud. But then I go back to what happened, and I think, no, this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. I’m sure of that. And the little signs — the trucks slowing down, the neighbors looking over, people seeming to know — those things proved to me that they knew it too.

So I guess the honest answer is, it would take a lot. More than someone disagreeing with me, more than a paper saying I’m wrong, more than people telling me I’m confused. Because I was the one who saw how it lined up.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F30.9, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because everybody made such a fuss about what I was doing at the house. They say I used up our savings buying wood and lights and all the display things, and I kept working on it even after it got dark. But to me, it didn’t feel foolish or wasteful. It felt important, like if I stopped, the house—and really the whole block—would miss its chance somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds like too much when I say it out loud, but at the time I just kept thinking it had to be finished, and I couldn’t just leave it half-done.\n\nDoctor: Did you believe that something bad would actually happen to the house or the block if you didn't finish the project?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like the roof was going to cave in that minute, if that’s what you mean. But it felt bigger than just boards and lights. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, my family especially, but I was sure this neighborhood had been picked for something. Like this was supposed to be my last big civic art piece, the thing I was meant to leave behind, and if I didn’t finish it then the whole block would miss what it was supposed to become.\n\nAnd there were signs, too. It wasn’t just in my head like people say. Trucks would slow down going by the house, not just one, several of them, and I could see the drivers looking. Neighbors would come out and stand there or look over from their yards like they knew something was happening but weren’t saying it directly. Those little things, they proved to me they understood the importance of it. So yes, I believed something bad would happen in the sense that the chance would be lost, like the place would fail at what it was chosen for.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong need to perform the project in a very specific way, or were there specific rituals you had to follow while working on it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it rituals exactly. I mean, I wasn’t doing some kind of… counting thing, or washing my hands over and over, or anything like that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I did feel like it had to be done right. The lights had to be in the right places, the wood had to line up a certain way, and if something looked off, I couldn’t just leave it. I’d step back and look at it from the street, then go back and change it, then look again. Sometimes I’d spend a long time on one section because it didn’t feel complete yet.\n\nSo, specific? Yes, in the sense that I had a picture in my head of how it was supposed to be, and I felt like I had to follow that. But not a ritual like I had a rule written down or had to repeat a certain thing three times. It was more like… I knew when it was wrong, and I knew I had to keep going until it matched what it was meant to be.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant distress or anxiety when you couldn't complete the project exactly as you had envisioned it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety exactly. I mean, I did get very worked up when something didn’t look right, or when someone interrupted me or told me to stop for the night. It wasn’t like a normal “oh, I’m frustrated” feeling. It felt like the whole thing was slipping out of my hands, like the picture I had in my mind was right there and if I didn’t fix that one section then and there, I might lose it.\n\nSo yes, I was upset when I couldn’t get it the way I saw it. I’d stand there looking at it, and it would bother me, like it was unfinished in a way other people couldn’t see. My family would say, “It’s fine, come inside,” but it didn’t feel fine to me. It felt wrong. Not just crooked or ugly, but wrong in a deeper way.\n\nBut I don’t know if distress is the right word either, because part of me felt driven, almost like I was close to making it what it needed to be. I wasn’t sitting there shaking or anything like that. I was more… agitated, I suppose. Restless. I couldn’t settle down if I knew a part of it wasn’t matching what I had in my head.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your beliefs about the project and its importance to the neighborhood might not be true?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a minute here and there, mostly because everybody kept telling me I was getting carried away. I mean, I know people think I’m mixed up about it. My family especially looked at me like I had gone too far, and they’d say things like, “It’s just the house,” or “Nobody else sees it that way.” So yes, I heard that. I understood what they were saying.\n\nBut inside, no… I didn’t really believe they were right. I was sure the neighborhood had been picked for it, like this was supposed to be my last big civic art piece, something I was meant to give the block before I’m gone or before I can’t do that kind of thing anymore. It didn’t feel like an idea I was just playing with. It felt like a fact I had finally understood.\n\nAnd it wasn’t only a feeling, either. There were signs. Trucks would slow down in front of the house, and not just once. I’d see the drivers looking over. Neighbors would come outside or look from their yards, like they knew something important was happening but didn’t want to say it out loud. Those things mattered to me. They proved, at least to me, that other people understood it too, even if later they acted like they didn’t.\n\nSo did I question it? Maybe briefly, when people pushed me. But I kept coming back to the same thing: this was important, and the block was supposed to become something through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that the neighborhood was chosen for this project and that it would miss its chance if you didn't finish it, despite what others have told you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I know how that sounds when I say it out loud, and I know people have told me I’m making too much of it. My family especially, they keep saying, “The neighborhood wasn’t chosen, it’s just you working on the house,” and I understand those words. I’m not deaf to it.\n\nBut no, I don’t really believe them. I still think this block was picked for it, or meant for it somehow. Not picked like someone sent me a letter, but picked in the way things line up and you finally see what they’re supposed to be. This was supposed to be my last big civic art piece, something for everybody here, not just for me. And if I don’t finish it, then yes, I do feel like the neighborhood misses the chance. Like the moment passes and people don’t even understand what they lost.\n\nAnd there were signs. It wasn’t just in my head the way they say. Trucks slowed down in front of the house. Drivers looked over more than normal. Neighbors came outside, stood in their yards, watched from their windows. They may not have said, “Yes, this is important,” but you can tell when people know something is happening. They knew. Maybe they didn’t want to admit it, or maybe they got embarrassed when my family started making it sound foolish, but I could see it.\n\nSo yes, despite what others told me, I still believe it. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but I’m sure this neighborhood was supposed to have this, and I was supposed to finish it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people in the neighborhood share your belief that the house and block would miss their chance if the project wasn't finished?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think some of them share it, even if they won’t come right out and say it. That’s the part my family doesn’t understand. They act like if nobody says the words, then nobody believes it, but people don’t always say important things plainly. Sometimes they show it by how they behave.\n\nLike I told you, the trucks slowing down — not just one truck, not just one time — drivers would look over at the house in that way. And neighbors, they’d be out in their yards, or standing near the sidewalk, or looking from the windows. You can tell when people are just being nosy, and you can tell when people sense something bigger is happening. This felt like the second one.\n\nNow, if you asked them directly, maybe they’d deny it. Maybe they’d say, “Oh no, I was just looking,” because people get embarrassed, or they don’t want to be pulled into family business. But I think they knew the block was being given a chance, and that if I didn’t finish it, the house and the neighborhood would lose something. I know people think I’m mixed up about that, but I’m sure this was meant to be my last big civic art piece, and I think the little signs showed that other people understood it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you misinterpreted the signs you mentioned, such as the trucks slowing down and the neighbors looking, and that they didn't actually understand or believe in the importance of your project?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is possible if you put it that way. I mean, I understand what you’re asking me — could the truck driver just have been slowing down because of the street, or looking because there was work going on, or the neighbor just happened to be outside? I know that’s the way my family explains it. They say, “People look at houses all the time,” and “You’re reading into it.” I hear that.\n\nBut no, I don’t really think I misinterpreted it. Not in the way they mean. It wasn’t one little thing by itself. It was the pattern of it. The timing. The way people would slow down right there, and look over, and the way neighbors seemed to come out and watch. It felt like they knew this was more than just fixing up a house. They may not have said it out loud, but I could tell they understood something important was happening.\n\nAnd I know people think I’m mixed up about it. I know that. I’m not saying I don’t know how it sounds. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for this, like it was meant to be my last big civic art piece for the block. Those little signs mattered. To me, they proved other people knew it too, even if they won’t admit it now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if maybe the whole thing isn’t real the way I’m saying it is. And I can understand why you’d ask that, because my family says it all the time — that I’m mixed up, that I’m putting meaning into ordinary things, that the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking me what I believe, no, I don’t really think it’s not based in reality. I think people just don’t see it, or they don’t want to say they see it. To me, it was too many little things lining up. The trucks slowing down in front of the house, the way the drivers looked over, the neighbors watching from their yards or windows — those weren’t nothing. That showed me they knew something was happening too.\n\nI know how that sounds. I’m not trying to be difficult. But I’m sure this neighborhood was picked, or meant, for my last big civic art piece. It wasn’t just me deciding that out of nowhere. The signs were there, and I still believe the block would miss its chance if I didn’t finish it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would change if everyone around you, including the neighbors and truck drivers, told you directly that they didn't understand its importance and weren't paying attention to it?\n\nPatient: If every single one of them said that? I mean… I’d hear them saying it. I’m not saying I’d cover my ears. If the neighbors came right out and said, “No, we weren’t watching for that,” or the truck drivers said, “No, I didn’t know anything about your project,” I would hear the words.\n\nBut I don’t think it would change what I know happened. Not completely. Because people can say things after the fact for all kinds of reasons. They don’t want to get involved, they don’t want to look strange, they don’t want to admit they understood something bigger was going on. Especially when my family is already saying I’m mixed up — people will go along with that because it’s easier.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just one person, Doctor. It was the whole feeling around the block. The slowing down, the looking over, the timing of it, the way it kept happening. That’s what people miss when they ask me like it’s just one neighbor staring out a window. It wasn’t that simple. It felt like the neighborhood had been picked for this, like the house and the block had a chance through my work.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think my belief would just go away because people denied it. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but I’m still sure this was meant to be my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, whether they admit it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would be considered strange or unrealistic by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know most people would probably say it sounds strange. I’m not blind to that. My family already says I’m mixed up about it, and I can hear how it sounds when I say the trucks slowing down or the neighbors looking over meant something. I know a regular person might say, “That’s just traffic,” or “People look at things,” or “Nobody picked the neighborhood for anything.”\n\nBut to me, that doesn’t mean it wasn’t real. It’s just that most people don’t look at the whole pattern the way I did. They take each little thing by itself and explain it away. It felt like they understood this was more than just some little project.\n\nSo yes, I know it would be considered unrealistic by a lot of people. I know that. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t admit it or don’t want to say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project could be a mistaken idea that is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me to leave room for the idea that I could be wrong… and I can say, in a general way, people can be wrong about things. I’m not saying I’ve never misunderstood anything in my life. But with this, no, I don’t really believe it’s just a mistaken idea.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. My family says that, and I can see by the way people react when I explain it that they think I’m reading too much into things. But it wasn’t just one little thing. It was the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, neighbors watching from windows or standing outside at just the right times. When it happens once, maybe you brush it off. When it keeps happening, and it lines up with what I was trying to do with the art piece, it means something.\n\nSo if you’re asking could it be not based in reality… I have a hard time saying yes to that, because to me the signs were there. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. Maybe other people don’t want to admit they understood it, or maybe they don’t see the pattern the way I do, but I still believe they knew something important was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any external evidence that could prove or disprove your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project?\n\nPatient: External evidence… well, that’s hard, because what counts as evidence to me doesn’t seem to count to other people.\n\nIf you mean some official paper saying, “Yes, this neighborhood was selected,” no, I don’t have a paper like that. Nobody handed me a certificate or came to the door and announced it. But that doesn’t mean there wasn’t evidence. The evidence was in what was happening around me — the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors being outside or watching at the right moments. Those are the things I keep coming back to, because they weren’t just random to me. They fit together.\n\nNow, could something disprove it? I suppose someone could try. They could say, “We checked, and nobody knew anything.” But I’ve already said, people can deny things. People don’t always admit when they notice something important, especially if they think they’ll be made to look foolish. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those things proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if a professional, such as an art critic or a community leader, told you that your work, although meaningful to you, does not have the broader significance you believe it does?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change it, no. I mean… I would listen, I’m not saying I’d put my hands over my ears. If an art critic or somebody from the community came and said, “Your work matters to you, but it doesn’t mean what you think it means,” I’d hear the words. But I don’t think that would prove they were right.\n\nBecause people can look at something and miss what’s really happening. Especially with a piece like this, where it wasn’t just the object or the painting or whatever they want to call it. It was the whole neighborhood, the way it was being seen, the way people were reacting around it. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t just me deciding one morning that my work was important. And I can understand why you’d ask that, because my family says it all the time — that I’m mixed up, that I’m putting meaning into ordinary things, that the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything.\n\nBut if you’re asking me what I believe, no, I don’t really think it’s not based in reality. I think people just don’t see it, or they don’t want to say they see it. To me, it was too many little things lining up. The trucks slowing down in front of the house, the way the drivers looked over, the neighbors watching from their yards or windows — those weren’t nothing. That showed me they knew something was happening too.\n\nI know how that sounds. I’m not trying to be difficult. But I’m sure this neighborhood was picked, or meant, for my last big civic art piece. It wasn’t just me deciding that out of nowhere. The signs were there, and I still believe the block would miss its chance if I didn’t finish it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would change if everyone around you, including the neighbors and truck drivers, told you directly that they didn't understand its importance and weren't paying attention to it?\n\nPatient: If every single one of them said that? I mean… I’d hear them saying it. I’m not saying I’d cover my ears. If the neighbors came right out and said, “No, we weren’t watching for that,” or the truck drivers said, “No, I didn’t know anything about your project,” I would hear the words.\n\nBut I don’t think it would change what I know happened. Not completely. Because people can say things after the fact for all kinds of reasons. They don’t want to get involved, they don’t want to look strange, they don’t want to admit they understood something bigger was going on. Especially when my family is already saying I’m mixed up — people will go along with that because it’s easier.\n\nAnd it wasn’t just one person, Doctor. It was the whole feeling around the block. The slowing down, the looking over, the timing of it, the way it kept happening. That’s what people miss when they ask me like it’s just one neighbor staring out a window. It wasn’t that simple. It felt like the neighborhood had been picked for this, like the house and the block had a chance through my work.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think my belief would just go away because people denied it. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but I’m still sure this was meant to be my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, whether they admit it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project would be considered strange or unrealistic by most people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know most people would probably say it sounds strange. I’m not blind to that. My family already says I’m mixed up about it, and I can hear how it sounds when I say the trucks slowing down or the neighbors looking over meant something. I know a regular person might say, “That’s just traffic,” or “People look at things,” or “Nobody picked the neighborhood for anything.”\n\nBut to me, that doesn’t mean it wasn’t real. It’s just that most people don’t look at the whole pattern the way I did. They take each little thing by itself and explain it away. But when you’re there, day after day, seeing the way people slow down right at the house, the way they glance over, the way neighbors seem to be watching from their yards or windows — it adds up. It felt like they understood this was more than just some little project.\n\nSo yes, I know it would be considered unrealistic by a lot of people. I know that. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t admit it or don’t want to say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project could be a mistaken idea that is not based in reality?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking me to leave room for the idea that I could be wrong… and I can say, in a general way, people can be wrong about things. I’m not saying I’ve never misunderstood anything in my life. But with this, no, I don’t really believe it’s just a mistaken idea.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. My family says that, and I can see by the way people react when I explain it that they think I’m reading too much into things. But it wasn’t just one little thing. It was the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, neighbors watching from windows or standing outside at just the right times. When it happens once, maybe you brush it off. When it keeps happening, and it lines up with what I was trying to do with the art piece, it means something.\n\nSo if you’re asking could it be not based in reality… I have a hard time saying yes to that, because to me the signs were there. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. Maybe other people don’t want to admit they understood it, or maybe they don’t see the pattern the way I do, but I still believe they knew something important was happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any external evidence that could prove or disprove your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for this project?\n\nPatient: External evidence… well, that’s hard, because what counts as evidence to me doesn’t seem to count to other people.\n\nIf you mean some official paper saying, “Yes, this neighborhood was selected,” no, I don’t have a paper like that. Nobody handed me a certificate or came to the door and announced it. But that doesn’t mean there wasn’t evidence. The evidence was in what was happening around me — the trucks slowing down right by the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors being outside or watching at the right moments. Those are the things I keep coming back to, because they weren’t just random to me. They fit together.\n\nNow, could something disprove it? I suppose someone could try. They could say, “We checked, and nobody knew anything.” But I’ve already said, people can deny things. People don’t always admit when they notice something important, especially if they think they’ll be made to look foolish. And once my family started saying I was mixed up, then of course people would back away from it.\n\nSo I know how it sounds. I know you’re asking for something outside my own mind, something you can put on a table and look at. But from where I was standing, the outside evidence was already there. It was the way the block moved around it, the timing, the attention, the little signs. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those things proved to me that other people knew it too, even if they won’t say it plainly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if a professional, such as an art critic or a community leader, told you that your work, although meaningful to you, does not have the broader significance you believe it does?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would change it, no. I mean… I would listen, I’m not saying I’d put my hands over my ears. If an art critic or somebody from the community came and said, “Your work matters to you, but it doesn’t mean what you think it means,” I’d hear the words. But I don’t think that would prove they were right.\n\nBecause people can look at something and miss what’s really happening. Especially with a piece like this, where it wasn’t just the object or the painting or whatever they want to call it. It was the whole neighborhood, the way it was being seen, the way people were reacting around it. That’s what I keep trying to explain. It wasn’t just me deciding one morning that my work was important. It was the trucks slowing down by the house, the drivers turning their heads, the neighbors looking out or standing around at the right times. Those things showed me people knew something was going on.\n\nSo if a professional told me it didn’t have broader significance, I’d probably think they either didn’t understand the full situation, or they were trying to talk me down because my family already said I’m mixed up. I know people think that about me. I know how it sounds. But I’m still sure the neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little signs proved to me that other people knew it too, whether they admit it or not.\n\nSo no… I don’t think one person’s opinion, even a professional person, would change what I know I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were shown clear evidence that contradicts them?\n\nPatient: If it was truly clear evidence… I mean, I hear what you’re saying. If somebody showed me something solid, something that wasn’t just somebody’s opinion, I’d have to look at it. I’m not trying to be impossible. I don’t want to sit here and say nothing could ever make me think.\n\nBut the problem is, when people say “clear evidence,” a lot of times they mean what they already believe. They mean my family saying I’m mixed up, or somebody telling me, “Those trucks were just driving by,” or “The neighbors were just outside.” And to me, that doesn’t answer it, because I was there. I saw how it happened. The trucks slowing right by the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching or coming out at the times that mattered — it wasn’t just one little thing. It was a pattern.\n\nSo if you showed me something that contradicted it, I’d probably want to know who made it, why they’re saying it, what they left out. Because people can deny things, or they can misunderstand what they saw. And once everybody starts saying I’m confused, then anything I say gets put in that box.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up, I do know that. But I’m still sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. Those little things that happened around it proved to me that people knew it too. So… I can say I would look at evidence, yes. But I don’t know that it would change what I know I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were shown clear evidence that the neighborhood was not, in fact, chosen for anything special?\n\nPatient: I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t think it would change it just because somebody showed me something that says the neighborhood wasn’t chosen. I’d have to look at what they were calling “clear evidence,” because that matters. A piece of paper or somebody saying, “No, nothing special was going on,” doesn’t erase what I saw with my own eyes.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up. I know my family and other people look at it like I connected things that weren’t connected. But I was there. It wasn’t just one truck one time, or one neighbor glancing out a window. It was the way things kept happening — trucks slowing down right near the house, drivers looking over like they knew, neighbors being outside or watching at just the right moments. Those little things added up. They showed me people knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if you showed me something saying, “No, the neighborhood wasn’t chosen for anything special,” I’d probably think maybe whoever wrote it didn’t understand what the project really was, or they were only looking for an official kind of choosing, like a committee or a permit or something. But that’s not the whole of it. The proof was in the attention around it, the timing, the way people acted.\n\nI’m not trying to be stubborn, Doctor. I know how this sounds. But no, I don’t think that kind of evidence would really change what I believe, because I know what I saw happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to experience a significant period of time, say several months, where none of the signs you mentioned, such as trucks slowing down or neighbors watching, occurred?\n\nPatient: Several months with none of it happening… I mean, I’d notice that, sure. I’m not saying I wouldn’t notice. If the trucks stopped slowing down, if the neighbors weren’t looking over anymore, if the whole feeling around the place went quiet, I’d be aware of that. I watch those things because that’s how I knew people understood what was going on.\n\nBut I don’t think it would make me say the neighborhood wasn’t picked. It might make me think that part of it had passed, or people were being more careful, or maybe once everybody knew I was onto it, they stopped showing it so openly. You know, signs don’t have to keep happening every single day for the thing to be real. A thing can happen, and then people back away from it.\n\nI know people think I’m mixed up about this. I know that. My family says it, and I can tell from the way people talk to me that they think I put too much meaning into ordinary things. But I’m telling you, when it was happening, it was not ordinary. Those trucks slowing down near the house, the drivers looking over, the neighbors watching at certain times — it all fit together. It proved to me that they knew this neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece.\n\nSo if months went by and there were no more signs, I might wonder why it got quiet. I might think the attention moved underground, or that people had already gotten the message. But no, I don’t think the quiet afterward would erase what I already saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project would change if you were to receive a professional evaluation stating that your perceptions of the neighborhood's reaction are not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Well… if a professional wrote that, I’d listen to it, I suppose. I’m not saying I’d throw it in the trash or anything like that. I know you all have training, and I know people are trying to make sense of what I’m saying. And I know people already think I’m mixed up about this — my family, neighbors, whoever — so a report like that wouldn’t surprise me exactly.\n\nBut would it change what I believe? I don’t think so. Not just because somebody professional says my perceptions aren’t based in reality. Because they weren’t standing there when it happened. They didn’t see the trucks slowing down by the house, the way the drivers looked over, and they didn’t see how the neighbors were watching or coming out at just the right times. It wasn’t one little thing I misunderstood. It was a whole pattern, and I lived through it.\n\nA professional evaluation might say, “He’s connecting things wrong,” or “Those were ordinary neighborhood things,” but that feels too easy to me. It puts everything in one box without really accounting for the timing and the feeling of it, the way it kept lining up around the project. I’m sure this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand. The little things proved people knew it too.\n\nSo I’d read it, or I’d hear it, and I might even try to think about it. But I don’t think it would take away what I know I saw. It would probably just make me feel like the professional didn’t have the whole picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about the project could be considered delusions by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I suppose a mental health professional could call them delusions. I understand that word gets used when somebody believes something other people don’t believe, or when people think the person is seeing meaning where there isn’t any. So if you’re asking whether someone in your position might write that down about me, then yes, I can see that happening.\n\nBut that doesn’t mean I agree with it. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. I know people think I’m mixed up. I know they hear me talk about the trucks slowing down, or the neighbors looking over, or people seeming to know when to be outside, and they think, “Well, he’s making too much of ordinary things.” I know how it sounds when it’s all put into words in an office like this.\n\nBut I was there. I saw how it was happening around the house. It wasn’t just one small thing. It was the pattern of it, the timing, the way it kept lining up. To me, those things proved that people knew the neighborhood had been picked for my last big civic art piece. So, yes, maybe a professional could call it that. I’m not blind to that. But in my mind, it feels like they’d be calling it that because they didn’t really see what I saw.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything could ever make you doubt your belief that the neighborhood was chosen for this project?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “ever” is a big word. I mean, I suppose if you’re asking me in a very plain way, could something make me question it, I can say maybe I would think about it if there was some real explanation for all of it that made sense. Not just somebody saying, “No, that didn’t happen,” because that doesn’t answer what I saw.\n\nThe thing is, I already know people think I’m mixed up about it. I’m not pretending I don’t know that. I can hear it in how my family talks to me, and I can hear it here too, the way the questions are asked. People think I took ordinary things — a truck slowing down, a neighbor looking over — and made them into something bigger. But to me it wasn’t just one truck, or one glance, or one person outside. It was the way all those little things came together around the house, around the neighborhood, around the idea of the project.\n\nSo could I doubt it? Maybe in little moments I could wonder why other people don’t see it the way I do. I can wonder why it sounds strange when I say it out loud. But then I go back to what happened, and I think, no, this neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece. I’m sure of that. And the little signs — the trucks slowing down, the neighbors looking over, people seeming to know — those things proved to me that they knew it too.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is, it would take a lot. More than someone disagreeing with me, more than a paper saying I’m wrong, more than people telling me I’m confused. Because I was the one who saw how it lined up."
